Lean and evidence guide
•
8 October 2026


How to use this volume
A guide to the actual formal development. 119 Lean source files. 151 map rows. 1,164 mapped
declarations. All 68 theorem-like manuscript results have full scoped coverage in the inherited
v1.0.0 formal release. Read the eight tutorial chapters for the mathematical route, the coverage
ledger for complete manuscript results, and the declaration catalog for exact source targets.
This edition explains that release and reconciles its source, contract, theorem map, and saved
kernel transcript. The formal execution evidence is dated 5 September 2026. No new Lean build
was run for this companion edition.

1 How to read this formalization
1.1 What has been completed
Paper IV studies one scalar cyclic family through several realizations. A residue-class projector selects
coefficients. Its exponential sections become entire functions. Signed sections produce a distinguished
denominator, whose reciprocal coefficients form parameterized polynomial families. The denomina-
tor’s zeros then control canonical products, a diagonal determinant, coefficient asymptotics, and zeta
functions.
The Lean release follows these transitions explicitly. It does not identify a formal series with an analytic
function by notation alone. It proves summation bridges, matches ordinary and factorial-normalized
coefficients, and carries zero multiplicities and logarithm choices into the analytic statements.
The completed formal contract has 68 full scoped manuscript results, zero partial, and zero un-
mapped. The 151 map rows name 1,164 distinct declarations: 953 theorems and 211 constructions.
The saved fail-closed namespace audit reports 2,234 declarations, including 1,999 theorem declara-
tions. These are different counts. The namespace includes supporting material beyond the explicit
map, while the map includes definitions as well as theorems. Neither is a count of independent math-
ematical discoveries.
1.2 Three levels of evidence

text are checked. This step does not compile Lean.
The transcript permits only the disclosed standard transitive axioms propext, Classical.choice, and
Quot.sound. This is a claim about the audited Lean environment and its declarations, not an assertion
of assumption-free mathematics. Hypotheses appear in declaration types and remain part of each
theorem.

This ordering is a reading route, not the exact import order. The final nine modules close 15 previously
partial manuscript records. They add 57 mapped declarations and connect earlier algebraic statements
to the analytic or literal displayed forms requested by the manuscript.
1.4 Reading a Lean declaration
An expression such as (hN : 0 < N) is an explicit hypothesis. Braces indicate implicit arguments whose
values Lean may infer. They do not erase hypotheses. A proposition such as HasSum f x asserts con-
vergence of the indexed sum to the displayed value. A bare tsum is an operation with a total definition
and does not, by itself, assert that a series converges. The same distinction applies to Multipliable and
an infinite product.
Formal power series, polynomials, complex functions, and continuous linear maps inhabit different
types. The transition between them is usually the mathematically important theorem. In this guide,
a short final proof often rests on substantial earlier modules. A two-line proof of the final Hadamard
identity is not the whole product argument.
The code excerpts are literal source intervals, including their proofs. They are reading excerpts in-
side the original module context, not independent files that can be pasted without imports, variables,
namespace declarations, or local instances. The complete source is supplied. The excerpt manifest
records each original file, line interval, file hash, excerpt hash, and display hash.
1.5 Boundaries that completion preserves
Imported standard fact 11.1 remains imported and is outside the 68-result contract. The checked order
proofs do not need it. The work does not claim universal simplicity of all zeros, positivity of every
reduced zero, a full displacement expansion, or a zeta functional equation. Eventual simplicity and
exact asymptotic statements are proved with their stated ranges. Nearest-pole results keep their integer
reciprocal-order and local pole hypotheses. Complex powers use a formal series, a normalized germ,
or one fixed holomorphic logarithm on the stated domain.
Paper IV’s diagonal determinant is a concrete scalar-family specialization. It is not the general bounded-
operator theory allocated to Paper VI, the arithmetic interpolation theory of Paper IX, or the abstract
completion questions of Paper X.

2 Coefficient algebra and normalization
2.1 The projector acts on coefficients
For a formal series 𝑓(𝑋) = ∑𝑛𝑎𝑛𝑋𝑛, the cyclic projector retains exactly those coefficients for which 𝑛≡
𝑗(mod 𝑁). The implementation uses the total natural-number remainder operation. Idempotence
makes sense without a positive-order assumption. The finite resolution over 0 ≤𝑗< 𝑁requires
𝑁> 0.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/CyclicFilters.lean.
def cyclicProjector (N j : ℕ) (f : R⟦X⟧) : R⟦X⟧:=
PowerSeries.mk fun n => if n % N = j % N then PowerSeries.coeff n f else 0
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/CyclicFilters.lean.
theorem cyclicProjector_idempotent (N j : ℕ) (f : R⟦X⟧) :
cyclicProjector N j (cyclicProjector N j f) = cyclicProjector N j f := by
ext n
by_cases h : n % N = j % N <;> simp [h]
The proof of idempotence is coefficientwise extensionality. After selecting an index, a case split de-
termines whether the residue matches. This is a universal identity over the source’s commutative-
semiring context, not a computation on a bounded list of coefficients.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/CyclicFilters.lean.
theorem sum_cyclicProjector {N : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) (f : R⟦X⟧) :
(∑j ∈Finset.range N, cyclicProjector N j f) = f := by
ext n
rw [map_sum]
rw [Finset.sum_eq_single (n % N)]
· simp [Nat.mod_eq_of_lt (Nat.mod_lt n hN)]
· intro j hj hne
have hjN : j < N := Finset.mem_range.mp hj
have hmod : j % N = j := Nat.mod_eq_of_lt hjN
have hres : n % N ≠j % N := by simpa [hmod] using hne.symm
simp [hres]
· exact fun hmem => (hmem (Finset.mem_range.mpr (Nat.mod_lt n hN))).elim
The positive-order hypothesis ensures that 𝑛mod 𝑁lies in the finite index set. The sum has exactly
one surviving term. This is also why the proof cannot simply discard the 𝑁= 0 boundary.
REALIZATION CALCULUS
3


OPERATION KOMOREBI
PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
2.2 Multiplication adds residue labels
A product of two pure packets has residue equal to the sum of their labels modulo 𝑁. The full product
law first reconstructs each factor from its packet sum, distributes the product, and projects every pair.
Nonmatching label pairs vanish. The displayed Lean proof records this route directly.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/CyclicFilters.lean.
theorem cyclic_product_law {N : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) (k : ℕ) (f g : R⟦X⟧) :
cyclicProjector N k (f * g) =
∑i ∈Finset.range N, ∑j ∈Finset.range N,
if (i + j) % N = k % N then
cyclicProjector N i f * cyclicProjector N j g
else 0 := by
calc
cyclicProjector N k (f * g) =
cyclicProjector N k
((∑i ∈Finset.range N, cyclicProjector N i f) *
(∑j ∈Finset.range N, cyclicProjector N j g)) := by
rw [sum_cyclicProjector hN, sum_cyclicProjector hN]
_ = ∑i ∈Finset.range N, ∑j ∈Finset.range N,
if (i + j) % N = k % N then
cyclicProjector N i f * cyclicProjector N j g
else 0 := by
rw [Finset.sum_mul]
simp_rw [Finset.mul_sum]
rw [cyclicProjector_sum]
apply Finset.sum_congr rfl
intro i hi
rw [cyclicProjector_sum]
apply Finset.sum_congr rfl
intro j hj
by_cases hlabel : (i + j) % N = k % N
· simp only [hlabel, ↓reduceIte]
rw [cyclicProjector_eq_of_mod_eq (i := k) (j := (i + j) % N)
(by simpa using hlabel.symm)]
exact cyclicProjector_product_packet N i j f g
· simp only [hlabel, ↓reduceIte]
exact cyclicProjector_product_other (R := R) (fun h => hlabel h.symm) f g
No analytic interchange occurs here. Every coefficient of a formal product is a finite convolution, and
the outer packet decomposition is finite. The pointwise counterpart, including covariance on invariant
domains, is supplied later by row 143.
2.3 Transfer and signed sections
The transfer and signed coefficient selectors are deliberately separated from their exponential-
generating normalization.
REALIZATION CALCULUS
4


OPERATION KOMOREBI
PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/SectionCycles.lean.
def transferSectionCoeff (N j : ℕ) (lambda : R) (n : ℕ) : R :=
if n % N = j then lambda ^ n else 0
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/SectionCycles.lean.
def signedSectionCoeff (N j : ℕ) (n : ℕ) : R :=
if n % N = j then (-1 : R) ^ (n / N) else 0
The transfer selector contributes 𝜆𝑛in one residue class. The signed selector contributes (−1)⌊𝑛/𝑁⌋.
The surrounding exponentialGeneratingSeries construction divides the 𝑛th selected coefficient by 𝑛!.
Thus the signed section is
𝐷𝑁,𝑗(𝑧) = ∑
𝑞≥0
(−1)𝑞𝑧𝑁𝑞+𝑗
(𝑁𝑞+ 𝑗)! ,
0 ≤𝑗< 𝑁.
Differentiating shifts the residue label downward. At label zero, crossing the period contributes a
minus sign.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/SectionCycles.lean.
theorem derivative_signedSection_zero {N : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) :
PowerSeries.derivative R (signedSection (R := R) N 0) =
-signedSection (R := R) N (N - 1) := by
rw [signedSection, derivative_exponentialGeneratingSeries, signedSection]
ext n
simp only [coeff_exponentialGeneratingSeries, map_neg]
rw [signedSectionCoeff_succ_zero hN]
ring
For a full period, the signed family solves the corresponding negative cyclic differential equation. The
formal derivative cycle is proved before the entire-function derivative cycle. The later analytic bridge
ensures that the same normalization appears in both.
2.4 Reduced and original variables
The distinguished denominator is the signed section with residue label zero in the original variable. It
is written in a reduced variable 𝑤= 𝑧𝑁through
𝐷𝑁(𝑧) = Δ𝑁(𝑧𝑁),
Δ𝑁(𝑤) = ∑
𝑛≥0
(−1)𝑛𝑤𝑛
(𝑁𝑛)! .
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/ReciprocalCore.lean.
def reducedDenominator (N : ℕ) : R⟦X⟧:=
PowerSeries.mk fun n =>
algebraMap ℚR ((-1 : ℚ) ^ n / ((N * n).factorial : ℚ))
The reciprocal family is generated formally by the negative logarithm of the unit series Δ𝑁. Its constant
coefficient is one, which is the algebraic condition needed to define the normalized logarithm and
reciprocal family.
REALIZATION CALCULUS
5


OPERATION KOMOREBI
PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/ReciprocalCore.lean.
def reciprocalSeries (N : ℕ) (r : R) : R⟦X⟧:=
formalExpFamily (negativeLogDenominator (R := R) N) r
Two normalizations coexist. Write
Δ𝑁(𝑤)−𝑟= ∑
𝑛≥0
𝑏𝑛(𝑟)𝑤𝑛= ∑
𝑛≥0
𝑃𝑁,𝑛(𝑟) 𝑤𝑛
(𝑁𝑛)!.
Then 𝑃𝑁,𝑛(𝑟) = (𝑁𝑛)!𝑏𝑛(𝑟). The source’s reciprocalOrdCoeff denotes the ordinary coefficient, while
reciprocalCoeff uses blockCoeff and the (𝑁𝑛)! normalization.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/ReciprocalCore.lean.
def reciprocalCoeff (N n : ℕ) (r : R) : R :=
blockCoeff N n (reciprocalSeries N r)
The parameter-addition law has binomial factors at the block indices, not ordinary (𝑛
𝑞) factors.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/ReciprocalCore.lean.
theorem reciprocalCoeff_addition (N n : ℕ) (r s : R) :
reciprocalCoeff N n (r + s) =
∑q ∈Finset.range (n + 1),
((N * n).choose (N * q) : R) *
reciprocalCoeff N q r * reciprocalCoeff N (n - q) s := by
exact reciprocal_block_convolution
(constantCoeff_negativeLogDenominator N) N n r s
Dividing this identity by (𝑁𝑛)! recovers the ordinary Cauchy convolution for the 𝑏𝑛. Multiplying by
the wrong factorial changes the theorem. The source makes that conversion an explicit named con-
struction.
2.5 Newton recurrence and its inputs
The logarithmic coefficients kappaCoeff encode the logarithmic derivative of the reduced denominator.
The formal differential identity for the reciprocal series gives a recurrence for its ordinary coefficients.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/ReciprocalCore.lean.
theorem reciprocalOrdCoeff_newton (N : ℕ) (r : R) (n : ℕ) :
(n + 1 : R) * reciprocalOrdCoeff N (n + 1) r =
r * ∑p ∈Finset.antidiagonal n,
reciprocalOrdCoeff N p.1 r * kappaCoeff N (p.2 + 1) := by
simpa [reciprocalOrdCoeff, kappaCoeff] using
reciprocalSeries_coefficient_recurrence (R := R) N r n
The antidiagonal index contains pairs with sum 𝑛. Its second component is shifted by one to match the
logarithmic derivative. This is the same recurrence later specialized to a literal Hessenberg determinant.
It is not necessary to assume that any zero sum converges at this stage. Zero moments are subsequently
proved equal to these algebraic coefficients.

6


OPERATION KOMOREBI
PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
2.6 Appell and theta families
The generic Appell polynomial is built from a formal exponential family and then differentiated as a
polynomial.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/Appell.lean.
def formalAppellPolynomial (L : R⟦X⟧) (r : R) (m : ℕ) : Polynomial R :=
appellPolynomial (fun q => egfCoeff q (formalExpFamily L r)) m
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/Appell.lean.
theorem derivative_formalAppellPolynomial (L : R⟦X⟧) (r : R) (m : ℕ) :
Polynomial.derivative (formalAppellPolynomial L r (m + 1)) =
Polynomial.C (m + 1 : R) * formalAppellPolynomial L r m := by
exact derivative_appellPolynomial _ m
The derivative law holds because polynomial differentiation is finite. The residue-specialized Appell
family also has parameter addition, covariance, a sparse coefficient formula, and an inverse differential-
operator action. Row 146 proves that the ordinary-derivative and factorial/Hasse encodings agree.
Both interpretations have an explicit bridge.
The theta construction combines two rescalings of the reciprocal family.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/ThetaCore.lean.
def thetaSeries (N : ℕ) (u : R) : R⟦X⟧:=
PowerSeries.rescale (-u ^ N) (reciprocalSeries N 1) *
PowerSeries.rescale (-1) (reciprocalSeries N u)
The rescaling factors −𝑢𝑁and −1 are part of the definition. Theta reconstruction, parameter factors,
polynomial degree, and Newton recurrence are covered by rows 22–23 and 43–45. A later analytic zero-
data formula identifies their coefficients with the corresponding Bell expressions. The formal identities
remain available without importing that analytic interpretation.

OPERATION KOMOREBI
PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
3 From formal series to entire functions
3.1 Finite exponential sums are the analytic realization
The entire transfer function is defined as a finite root-weighted exponential sum.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/EntireBridge.lean.
def entireTransfer (N j : ℕ) (omega lambda : ℂ) (z : ℂ) : ℂ:=
(N : ℂ)⁻¹ *
∑l ∈Finset.range N,
((omega ^ j)⁻¹) ^ l * Complex.exp ((lambda * omega ^ l) * z)
The primitive root, residue index, and spectral parameter appear explicitly. The finite sum is entire
because each exponential summand is entire. The crucial next step is a HasSum theorem identifying its
Taylor coefficients with the formal transfer section.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/EntireBridge.lean.
theorem hasSum_entireTransfer {N j : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) (hj : j < N)
{omega : ℂ} (homega : IsPrimitiveRoot omega N) (lambda z : ℂ) :
HasSum (fun n => PowerSeries.coeff n (transferSection N j lambda) * z ^ n)
(entireTransfer N j omega lambda z) := by
simpa only [finiteRootAverage_rescale_exp_eq_transferSection hN hj homega]
using hasSum_finiteRootAverage N j omega lambda z
This theorem has positive order, a residue index in range, and a primitive root as hypotheses. It pro-
vides a convergent series at every complex argument. It also permits independence-of-root proofs: two
finite sums obtained from allowed root choices have the same summing series, hence the same value
by uniqueness of a sum.
The signed analytic realization additionally chooses 𝜌with 𝜌𝑁= −1. The factor 𝜌−𝑗compensates
for the residue label. It cannot be removed without changing the normalization of the basis. The
independent-root statements compare the resulting functions under exactly the root assumptions they
require.
3.2 Initial data and Wronskians
The formal cycle specifies derivatives at zero, while the analytic bridge transfers them to iterated com-
plex derivatives. For the normalized signed basis, the initial-data matrix is the identity. Constancy of
the Wronskian then identifies the Wronskian with one for the stated 𝑁> 1 range.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/Wronskians.lean.
theorem signedWronskian_eq_one {N : ℕ} (hN : 1 < N) {omega rho : ℂ}
(homega : IsPrimitiveRoot omega N) (hrho : rho ^ N = -1) (z : ℂ) :
analyticWronskian (fun j : Fin N => entireSigned N j.val omega rho) z = 1 := by
rw [signedWronskian_constant hN homega]
have hm : (fun k j : Fin N => deriv^[k.val]
(entireSigned N j.val omega rho) 0) = (1 : Matrix (Fin N) (Fin N) ℂ) := by
ext k j

rw [entireSigned_initial_data (by omega) j.isLt k.isLt homega hrho]
simp [Matrix.one_apply, Fin.ext_iff]
rw [analyticWronskian, hm, Matrix.det_one]
The source does not conclude linear independence from an informal appeal to distinct exponentials
alone. It factors the exponential jet matrix, handles its determinant, proves the Wronskian law, and
identifies the normalized initial matrix. The ODE modules then describe the corresponding solution-
space realization.
3.3 Companion matrices and finite algebras
The signed shift matrix has a negative carry across the cyclic boundary. Its powers encode paths
through the residue labels. This provides a companion realization of the signed differential cycle,
explicit fundamental-matrix entries, addition and inversion identities, and a spectrum controlled by
the chosen roots.
There are two compatible algebra models. In the cyclic quotient algebra the generator satisfies 𝑥𝑁= 1.
In the signed quotient it satisfies 𝑥𝑁= −1. Earlier modules construct the quotient algebras and their
power bases, then prove formal exponential identities. Row 151 equips the finite-dimensional quo-
tients with coefficient norm topologies and proves convergence of the actual exponential series. Con-
sequently, the analytic finite-algebra identity is more than equality of formal coefficient expressions.
3.4 Why the final pointwise addition theorem matters
A bivariate formal-series addition law is an algebraic identity among coefficients. It does not on its own
prove equality of evaluated functions at arbitrary complex arguments. The completed source includes
both. Row 144 obtains the actual transfer and signed addition formulas, retaining the signed carry
factor forced by 𝜌𝑁= −1.
The useful reading order is: formal product and addition identities, the entire summation bridge, then
the pointwise closure. Keeping these stages visible prevents a notation change from hiding the conver-
gence theorem. It also makes the formal statements reusable in contexts where no analytic topology
has been chosen.
3.5 Growth order and indicator geometry
The entire functions are finite exponential sums. Their exponents form regular polygon configurations
in the complex plane. In directions with a unique exposed vertex, one exponential dominates. On tie
directions, cancellation can obstruct a naive pointwise lower bound, so the exact indicator proof uses
aligned radii in addition to uniform sector estimates.
Rows 110–115 prove sharp order, exponential type, indicator geometry, uniform dominance in sectors,
and the exact directional indicator. These are proved source routes. Imported standard fact 11.1 re-
mains recorded separately and is not required for the checked order proofs. The guide therefore does
not count that imported fact among the 68 completed results.

4 Zero geometry, exhaustion, and counting
4.1 Local zeros are only the start
The two-term dominance analysis separates the leading pair of exponentials from a controlled remain-
der. Near the cancellation lattice of the leading pair, the perturbation argument constructs nearby
zeros and estimates their displacement. The remainder bounds include derivative information, which
supports simplicity and the first correction term.
A local construction alone would not justify counting all zeros. The source therefore adds sector con-
finement, sector exhaustion, rotation, and global exhaustion. Those modules show that sufficiently
large zeros belong to the constructed symmetry strings.
Finite exceptional zeros remain present
throughout the later counting and product arguments.

4.2 Named enumeration is constructed, not assumed
The SignedZeroEnumeration structure packages a positive simple tail, its lattice windows, rotation orbits,
and the finite-complement information needed downstream. Existence of this structure is proved for
every 𝑁≥2, with the exact order-two cosine model handled separately.
A downstream theorem may accept an enumeration as input because it is reusable for any structure
with the required fields. That is different from assuming the final zero theorem. The manuscript spe-
cializations instantiate it using canonicalZeroEnumeration, constructed from the preceding analytic
results. To audit a final theorem, follow that constructor as well as the generic lemma.
4.3 Multiplicity enters the actual counting function
The disk counting function sums analyticOrderNatAt over the actual finite zero set. The source proves
that these analytic orders are finite for the nonzero entire denominators. Thus replacing an extended
order by its natural-number value does not silently discard an infinite order.
The full original-variable counting theorem keeps every exceptional multiplicity:

The proof compares the full count with the simple tail count and adds a finite exception weight. The
final error constant contains both that weight and the tail shift. It is uniform for all nonnegative radii,
not only an eventual asymptotic abbreviation.
The corresponding reduced-variable count is
𝑛Δ𝑁(𝑅) = sin(𝜋/𝑁)
𝜋
𝑅1/𝑁+ 𝑂(1),
𝑁≥2.
The original-variable constant is 𝑁sin(𝜋/𝑁)/𝜋. These constants differ because one original symmetry
orbit collapses to one reduced zero and the radius changes by the 𝑁th power. Both transformations
are proved in the counting modules.
4.4 Sharp summability thresholds
Rows 72 and 79 distinguish a tail estimate from the full theorem. The full reciprocal-power sums range
over actual zeros with their analytic multiplicities. Their sharp thresholds are 𝑠> 1 for the original
denominator and 𝑠> 1/𝑁for the reduced denominator. The boundary divergences are included.
Removing finite exceptions is justified by a proved summability equivalence, not by silently redefining
the zero set.
For 𝑁≥2, reduced reciprocal zeros are absolutely summable because 1 > 1/𝑁. This precise conse-
quence feeds the genus-zero product and the trace-class diagonal model. It would fail as a route at
𝑁= 1, where the denominator is exponential and the analytic situation is handled separately.
REALIZATION CALCULUS
11


OPERATION KOMOREBI
PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
4.5 What eventual simplicity leaves open
The source proves eventual simplicity and exact rays for sufficiently large zeros. It does not replace
this with a blanket assertion that all zeros are simple. A finite exceptional zero can have multiplicity
greater than one, and the canonical product includes it accordingly. A finite exceptional zero can also
lie outside the asymptotic positive-ray description. The global product and zero-zeta constructions are
designed to retain such exceptions.
The first displacement correction and its exponential error are likewise specific proved statements.
They do not constitute an all-orders expansion. The explicit cubic correction in row 84 is a special-
ization with exact spectral gap, lattice centers, and phase, not a numerical fit to plotted zeros.

5 Canonical products, moments, and the diagonal model
5.1 The index set records multiplicity
The product index is a dependent pair. Its first component chooses an actual zero. Its second chooses
one of the copies counted by that zero’s analytic multiplicity.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/FullCanonicalProduct.lean.
def ZeroMultiplicityIndex (f : ℂ→ℂ) :=
Σ z : {z : ℂ// f z = 0}, Fin (analyticOrderNatAt f z)
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/FullCanonicalProduct.lean.
def fullCanonicalProduct (f : ℂ→ℂ) (w : ℂ) : ℂ:=
∏' i : ZeroMultiplicityIndex f, (1 - w / indexedZero i)
For a zero of order 𝑚, the dependent finite type contributes exactly 𝑚copies of its factor. The construc-
tion therefore does not require a global simplicity assumption. Its factor uses the reduced variable
𝑤.
5.2 Absolute summability precedes the product theorem
The weighted reciprocal-zero sum is converted to a sum over the multiplicity index.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/FullCanonicalProduct.lean.
theorem zeroMultiplicity_reciprocal_summable {f : ℂ→ℂ} (hf : ZeroPowerSummable f 1) :
Summable (fun i : ZeroMultiplicityIndex f => 1 / ‖indexedZero i‖) := by
apply (summable_sigma_of_nonneg (fun i : ZeroMultiplicityIndex f => by positivity)).mpr
constructor
· intro z
exact (hasSum_fintype _).summable
· simpa only [indexedZero, tsum_fintype, Finset.sum_const, Finset.card_univ,
Fintype.card_fin, nsmul_eq_mul, mul_one_div, ZeroPowerSummable, Real.rpow_one] using hf
The next source results prove that the perturbations 𝑤/𝜌are absolutely summable, the product is multi-
pliable, convergence is uniform on compact sets and locally uniform, and the product defines an entire
function. These are needed analytic statements. Writing an infinite product symbol does not establish
them.
The product’s zeros and multiplicities are then identified. Dividing the denominator by the product
gives an entire, nonvanishing quotient. Growth estimates and a sublinear rigidity argument force the
normalized quotient to be one. Only after those steps is the final identity short:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/HadamardIdentification.lean.
theorem entireReducedDenominator_eq_fullCanonicalProduct {N : ℕ} (hN : 2 ≤N) :
entireReducedDenominator N = fullCanonicalProduct (entireReducedDenominator N) := by
funext z
have he := (entireReducedDenominator_product_quotient hN).2.2.2 z
rw [entireReducedDenominator_quotient_eq_one hN, Pi.one_apply, mul_one] at he
exact he.symm

The proof depends on the product-quotient theorem and the quotient-equals-one theorem. Those in
turn depend on convergence, exact multiplicities, growth reduction, and Jensen–Poisson estimates.
Rows 96–102 expose these stages in the map. The earlier tail product in row 74 is one ingredient, not
the complete identification.
5.3 Recovering coefficients from zeros
After the full product is identified, its logarithmic derivative gives a convergent Mittag–Leffler expan-
sion away from the zeros. Rows 104–109 match derivatives and analytic Taylor coefficients with the
actual multiplicity-weighted zero moments. The coefficients previously called kappaCoeff are thereby
identified with zero sums.
This direction matters. The coefficient recurrence was proved algebraically before any product formula.
The analytic argument does not define the denominator’s coefficients by a conjectured zero expansion.
It proves that the already-defined coefficients equal the zero data. Tangent coefficients, reciprocal Bell
formulas, and theta Bell formulas then inherit exact normalizations through named bridges.
Normalized nonzero residue sections need their own growth and zero arguments. Rows 121–132 cover
their entire realization, zero-growth estimates, summability, product identification, logarithms, ana-
lytic powers, and Taylor-differential conversions. They are not inferred merely by relabeling the distin-
guished denominator.
5.4 A concrete trace-class definition
The source uses a rank-one summability definition for Hilbert trace class.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/DiagonalNuclear.lean.
def IsHilbertTraceClass {E : Type*} [NormedAddCommGroup E] [InnerProductSpace ℂE]
(T : E →L[ℂ] E) : Prop :=
∃(ι : Type) (x y : ι →E), Summable (fun i => ‖x i‖ * ‖y i‖) ∧
HasSum (fun i => rankOne ℂ(x i) (y i)) T
The witness is a family of rank-one operators with summable products of vector norms and a sum equal
to the continuous linear map. For the diagonal model, reciprocal reduced zeros form the eigenvalue
family. Their absolute summability is proved from the zero theorem, then used to build the operator
and its trace-class witness.
The source also proves the basis action, power traces, finite compression determinants, and their limit.
The exact determinant identity is:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/DiagonalFredholm.lean.
theorem reducedZeroOperator_fredholm {N : ℕ} (hN : 2 ≤N) (w : ℂ) :
diagonalFredholmDeterminant (reducedZeroEigenvalue N) w = entireReducedDenominator N w := by
calc
_ = fullCanonicalProduct (entireReducedDenominator N) w := by
simp only [diagonalFredholmDeterminant, reducedZeroEigenvalue, fullCanonicalProduct,
div_eq_mul_inv]
↪
_ = _ := congrFun (entireReducedDenominator_eq_fullCanonicalProduct hN).symm w

Here diagonalFredholmDeterminant is the defined diagonal specialization, backed by finite-
compression convergence. The identity follows by matching its factors to the multiplicity-indexed
canonical product. It does not assert a general Fredholm determinant theory for arbitrary operators
or conceal such a theorem behind the name.
5.5 Complete symmetric functions
The reciprocal of the diagonal determinant has complete homogeneous symmetric-function coeffi-
cients. Rows 119–120 handle the finite combinatorics and the infinite summability argument. The
latter cannot be replaced by an unqualified passage from finite-dimensional symmetric polynomials
to infinitely many variables. The summable eigenvalue family is the analytic input that makes the
coefficient expression meaningful.
This yields three linked descriptions of the same scalar family: formal reciprocal coefficients, zero mo-
ments and symmetric functions, and a diagonal determinant model. Each identification is a theorem
with a specified source and normalization.

6 Pole coefficients and zero zeta functions
6.1 Nearest-pole estimates retain a local analytic contract
For a positive integral reciprocal order, a simple denominator zero produces a pole of finite order.
The source constructs principal parts, proves their uniqueness, removes them, and bounds the Taylor
coefficients of the analytic remainder. This produces the nearest-circle coefficient expansion with a
controlled error.
The generic coefficient bound below states its local principal-part assumption explicitly. Later source
results construct the required principal parts for the manuscript specialization.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/NearestPoleExpansion.lean.
theorem reciprocalCoeff_nearest_pole_bound {N : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N)
{S : Finset ℂ} (hS : ∀ρ ∈S, ρ ≠0) (a : ℂ→ℕ→ℂ) (r : ℕ)
{R R₂ : ℝ} (hR : 0 < R) (hR₂ : R < R₂)

(hlocal : ∀ρ ∈S, ∃C : ℝ, ∀ᶠz in 𝓝[≠] ρ,

∃C : ℝ, ∀n : ℕ,

∑ρ ∈S, ∑k ∈Finset.range r, a ρ (k+1) * ((N*n+k).choose k : ℂ) * ρ⁻¹ ^ (N*n)‖ ≤

hS a r hR hR₂ hzeros hlocal

The finite set 𝑆contains the zeros inside the larger disk. The inner radius 𝑅is strictly smaller than the
outer radius 𝑅2. The assumption hlocal states that after subtracting the proposed principal part, the
function is locally bounded near each selected pole. These are the hypotheses needed by the removable-
singularity and Taylor-bound route.
The conclusion is in the manuscript’s block normalization. Its leading sum and error both carry (𝑁𝑛)!.
The factor 𝜌−𝑁𝑛arises from extracting the original-variable coefficient at degree 𝑁𝑛. The binomial co-
efficient records the higher pole order. Omitting any of these factors changes the asymptotic statement.
6.2 Principal parts are constructed before the final specialization
Rows 133–138 provide coefficient bounds, principal-part coefficients, pole removal, Taylor recovery,
simple-pole expansions, and uniqueness. Row 139 supplies the full nearest-pole expansion. Row 140
isolates the leading term under its stated dominance hypotheses. Row 141 reduces symmetry orbits on
the nearest circle. Row 142 gives the secant specialization.
This route is conditional in the mathematically ordinary sense that the theorem retains its hypotheses
on reciprocal order, selected zeros, radii, and simplicity where used. Formal completion proves those
scoped statements. It does not delete the assumptions or claim that every parameter and every zero
configuration satisfies them.

6.3 Absolute zero zeta and branch-dependent zeta differ
The absolute zero zeta uses terms of the form
𝑚(𝜌) exp(−𝑠log |𝜌|),
where 𝑚(𝜌) is analytic multiplicity and the real logarithm of the norm is unambiguous. The reduced
denominator has sharp absolute-convergence half-plane Re 𝑠> 1/𝑁. The original denominator has
Re 𝑠> 1. Local uniform convergence and holomorphy are proved, as is failure of absolute convergence
on and to the left of the respective boundaries.
A branch-dependent symmetry-orbit zeta instead assigns complex logarithms. Its terms carry argu-
ment information. The source requires compatible logarithm choices and records the phase factors in
its continuation. These are related constructions, not interchangeable notations for one function.
6.4 Continuation is an explicit model plus an entire correction
Exponential displacement of the positive zero sequence from a lattice makes the difference of the two
zeta sums entire. The source proves a locally uniform majorant on bounded sets of the parameter. It
then constructs the lattice correction, finite missing lattice prefix, and finite exceptional-zero contribu-
tion.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/ZeroZetaContinuation.lean.
def reducedZetaCorrection {N : ℕ} (hN : 2 ≤N) (s : ℂ) : ℂ:=
let e := canonicalZeroEnumeration hN
e.reducedExceptionZeta (by omega) s + e.latticeCorrection ((N : ℂ) * s) -
e.latticePrefix ((N : ℂ) * s)
The finite exception term retains multiplicity. The correction and prefix use the scaled parameter 𝑁𝑠
because reduced roots are 𝑁th powers of the positive original roots. The equality with the Hurwitz
lattice model is proved in the initial convergence domain:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/ZeroZetaContinuation.lean.
theorem entireReducedDenominator_zeta_eq_hurwitz {N : ℕ} (hN : 2 ≤N)

absoluteZeroZeta (entireReducedDenominator N) s =
hurwitzLatticeModel (Real.pi / Real.sin (Real.pi / N)) N s + reducedZetaCorrection hN s :=

The continuation is then defined by the meromorphic model plus its entire correction.

theorem reducedZetaContinuation_meromorphic {N : ℕ} (hN : 2 ≤N) (s : ℂ) :
MeromorphicAt (reducedZetaContinuation hN) s :=
(hurwitzLatticeModel_meromorphicAt _ _ s).add
((reducedZetaCorrection_entire hN).analyticAt s).meromorphicAt
This theorem is short because its two analytic ingredients have already been proved. The continuation
is tied to the original sum by the preceding equality. The source also proves holomorphy off the pole,
exact residues, and meromorphic order −1 at the nonzero-residue pole. It does not merely define a
meromorphic function and call it a continuation.
6.5 Derivative weights and boundary orders
Derivative-weighted sums use complex derivative values, not only their moduli. A lower bound on
the derivative at sufficiently large zeros gives exponential decay of its inverse powers. For 𝑁≥3 and
positive integral derivative weight, this dominates every fixed polynomial weight and yields an entire
sum, assuming logarithm arguments remain in a fixed bounded interval.
The order-two case is separate: the derivative magnitude does not provide the same exponential gain,
and the theorem retains a right half-plane of convergence. Rows 85–87 state and prove this distinction.
Exact order-two and order-four zeta models are separately identified in rows 94–95.

7 The nine modules that close the manuscript contract
7.1 Row 143: actual pointwise projectors
The earlier coefficient projector and formal Fourier average are joined to the literal finite average on
functions.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/PointwiseFilters.lean.
def pointwiseRootAverage (N j : ℕ) (omega : ℂ) (f : ℂ→ℂ) (z : ℂ) : ℂ:=
(N : ℂ)⁻¹ * ∑l ∈Finset.range N, ((omega ^ j)⁻¹) ^ l * f (omega ^ l * z)
The projector law is proved for actual pointwise values:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/PointwiseFilters.lean.
theorem pointwiseRootAverage_projector {N : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) {omega : ℂ}
(ho : IsPrimitiveRoot omega N) (i j : ℕ) (f : ℂ→ℂ) (z : ℂ) :
pointwiseRootAverage N i omega (pointwiseRootAverage N j omega f) z =
if j % N = i % N then pointwiseRootAverage N j omega f z else 0 := by
exact pointwiseRootAverage_of_covariant hN ho (mapsTo_univ _ _) i j _
(fun w _ => pointwiseRootAverage_covariant hN ho j f w) (mem_univ z)
On an invariant domain, the range characterization also tracks differentiability and equality restricted
to that domain.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/PointwiseFilters.lean.
theorem pointwiseRootAverage_range_iff {N : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) {omega : ℂ}
(ho : IsPrimitiveRoot omega N) {s : Set ℂ}
(hs : MapsTo (fun z => omega * z) s s) (j : ℕ) {f : ℂ→ℂ}
(hf : DifferentiableOn ℂf s) :
(∃g : ℂ→ℂ, DifferentiableOn ℂg s ∧EqOn (pointwiseRootAverage N j omega g) f s) ↔
∀z ∈s, f (omega * z) = omega ^ j * f z := by
constructor
· rintro ⟨g, _, hg⟩z hz
rw [←hg (hs hz), ←hg hz]
exact pointwiseRootAverage_covariant hN ho j g z
· intro hc
refine ⟨f, hf, fun z hz => ?_⟩
simpa using pointwiseRootAverage_of_covariant hN ho hs j j f hc hz
The domain condition says multiplication by the primitive root maps the set into itself. This is needed to
apply covariance repeatedly along an orbit without evaluating a domain-restricted hypothesis outside
its scope. The proof of the reverse implication chooses the function itself as a preimage. The forward
implication uses covariance of the finite root average. Row 143 also includes the full pointwise product
law and coefficient-series summation, closing the formal-to-holomorphic clauses of results 3.1–3.3.

7.2 Row 144: arbitrary-argument addition
PointwiseAddition supplies the actual entire transfer addition theorem and the signed addition the-
orem for arbitrary complex arguments. The signed carry is derived from the relation 𝜌𝑁= −1. This
completes results 4.4 and 5.5 together with their earlier formal rows. No unspecified analytic evalua-
tion map is left between the bivariate formal identity and the pointwise equality.
7.3 Row 145: one fixed logarithm on the domain
A complex reciprocal power needs a logarithm choice. The source makes it part of the data:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/AnalyticReciprocalLaws.lean.
def IsLogarithmOn (f L : ℂ→ℂ) (s : Set ℂ) : Prop :=
DifferentiableOn ℂL s ∧∀z ∈s, Complex.exp (L z) = f z
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/AnalyticReciprocalLaws.lean.
def logarithmPower (L : ℂ→ℂ) (r : ℂ) (z : ℂ) : ℂ:=
Complex.exp (-r * L z)
For one chosen 𝐿, the parameter law follows directly from the exponential addition identity.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/AnalyticReciprocalLaws.lean.
theorem logarithmPower_add (L : ℂ→ℂ) (r t z : ℂ) :
logarithmPower L r z * logarithmPower L t z = logarithmPower L (r + t) z := by
unfold logarithmPower
rw [←Complex.exp_add]
congr 1
ring
Differentiation requires an open domain and a holomorphic logarithm of the actual denominator.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/AnalyticReciprocalLaws.lean.
theorem reciprocal_fixed_log_derivative {N : ℕ} {L : ℂ→ℂ} {s : Set ℂ}
(hs : IsOpen s) (hL : IsLogarithmOn (canonicalSignedDenominator N) L s)
(r : ℂ) {z : ℂ} (hz : z ∈s) :
deriv (logarithmPower L r) z =
r * analyticTransferTangent N z * logarithmPower L r z :=
(hasDerivAt_logarithmPower hs hL r hz).deriv
The source also proves the differential Bell/Riccati identity at points where the denominator is nonzero.
These statements close results 7.4, 8.4, and 8.5 alongside their formal coefficient laws. They do not
choose unrelated logarithms for different parameters and then assert a branch-independent multiplica-
tive law.

7.4 Row 146: ordinary derivatives and Hasse derivatives
The formal differential operator acts on polynomials, so its apparently infinite series truncates. There
are two encodings: ordinary iterated derivatives and factorial-normalized Hasse derivatives.
The
bridge is explicit:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/OperatorEncodingBridge.lean.
theorem truncatedDifferentialOperator_egfAction (A : R⟦X⟧) (p : Polynomial R) :
truncatedDifferentialOperator p.natDegree A p =
egfConstantCoeffAction (fun q => (q.factorial : R) * PowerSeries.coeff q A) p := by
rw [truncatedDifferentialOperator_apply, egfConstantCoeffAction]
apply Finset.sum_congr rfl
intro q _
have h := congrFun (Polynomial.factorial_smul_hasseDeriv (R := R) q) p
change q.factorial • (Polynomial.hasseDeriv q p) = Polynomial.derivative^[q] p at h
rw [←h]
simp only [nsmul_eq_mul, map_mul, map_natCast]
ring
The proof uses the identity relating the 𝑞th Hasse derivative to the ordinary 𝑞th derivative after mul-
tiplication by 𝑞!. The source then proves the forward action on the named residue Appell polynomial
and its inverse.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/OperatorEncodingBridge.lean.
theorem residueAppell_operator_inverse (N j : ℕ) (hN : 0 < N) (r : R) (m : ℕ) :
truncatedDifferentialOperator m
(PowerSeries.expand N hN.ne' (residuePowerSeries N j (-r)))
(residueAppellPolynomialParam N j hN r m) = Polynomial.X ^ m := by
rw [←residueAppell_operator_forward N j hN r m]
exact truncatedDifferentialOperator_residuePowerSeries_inverse N j hN r m
(by simp)
The inverse parameter is −𝑟. The expansion of the residue series changes the differential powers by
multiples of 𝑁. The polynomial degree makes the action finite, so the result does not require a Banach-
space operator convergence theorem.
7.5 Row 147: the order-one tangent boundary
The order-one denominator is identified exactly:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/TangentBoundary.lean.
theorem canonicalSignedDenominator_one (z : ℂ) :
canonicalSignedDenominator 1 z = Complex.exp (-z) := by
have h := entireSigned_independent_roots (N := 1) (j := 0) (by omega) (by omega)
(canonicalPrimitiveRoot_isPrimitive (by omega)) IsPrimitiveRoot.one
(canonicalSignedRoot_pow (by omega)) (show (-1 : ℂ) ^ 1 = -1 by simp) z
change canonicalSignedDenominator 1 z = _ at h
simpa [entireSigned, entireTransfer] using h
It follows that the transfer tangent is one and its coefficients have the corresponding degenerate form.
This boundary case is joined to the earlier 𝑁≥2 convergence theorem to obtain every positive order.

Source: KomorebiPaperIV/TangentBoundary.lean.
theorem analyticTransferTangent_hasSum_all_orders {N : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) {R : ℝ}
(hn : ∀w ∈Metric.ball (0 : ℂ) R, entireReducedDenominator N w ≠0)
{z : ℂ} (hz : z ^ N ∈Metric.ball (0 : ℂ) R) :
HasSum (fun n => transferTangentCoeff (R := ℂ) N n /
((N * n + N - 1).factorial : ℂ) * z ^ (N * n + N - 1))
(analyticTransferTangent N z) := by
by_cases h : N = 1
· subst N
exact analyticTransferTangent_one_hasSum z
· exact analyticTransferTangent_coefficient_hasSum (by omega) hn hz
Notice the original-variable exponent 𝑁𝑛+ 𝑁−1 and factorial (𝑁𝑛+ 𝑁−1)!. The nonvanishing
hypothesis is stated in the reduced-variable ball, while the evaluation point is tested through 𝑧𝑁. These
are explicit normalizations, not cosmetic choices.
7.6 Row 148: literal compact reciprocal polynomials
A recurrence determines low-order coefficients, but the manuscript displays compact binomial/facto-
rial expressions. This module proves the displayed forms over every characteristic-zero field. For the
second coefficient:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/CompactReciprocal.lean.
theorem reciprocalCoeff_two_compact {N : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) (r : K) :
reciprocalCoeff N 2 r =
r * (((2*N).choose N : K) / 2 * (r+1) - 1) := by
have h := (ordinary_low_equations N r).2.1
change 2 * PowerSeries.coeff 2 (reciprocalSeries N r) = _ at h
rw [reciprocalCoeff, blockCoeff, Nat.mul_comm N 2,
Nat.cast_choose K (show N ≤2*N by omega), show 2*N-N=N by omega]
have hf : ((2*N).factorial : K) ≠0 := by exact_mod_cast (2*N).factorial_ne_zero
have hg : (N.factorial : K) ≠0 := by exact_mod_cast N.factorial_ne_zero
field_simp at h ⊢
linear_combination h
The third coefficient is likewise identified by its literal formula. The proof moves between ordinary co-
efficients and factorial normalization, justifies the nonzero factorial denominators, and uses the earlier
recurrence. This closes the displayed-polynomial clause rather than relying on an informal simplifica-
tion outside Lean.
7.7 Row 149: the displayed Hessenberg determinant
The determinant’s matrix is defined entry by entry. Below the diagonal it contains the recurrence
coefficients. Its superdiagonal contains negative integers.
REALIZATION CALCULUS
22


OPERATION KOMOREBI
PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/NewtonDeterminant.lean.
def newtonHessenberg (p : ℕ→K) (n : ℕ) : Matrix (Fin n) (Fin n) K :=
fun i j => (if j.val ≤i.val then p (i.val+1-j.val) else 0) -
(if j.val = i.val+1 then (i.val+1 : K) else 0)
The generic determinant theorem identifies the determinant with 𝑛!𝑏𝑛. The reciprocal specialization
is:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/NewtonDeterminant.lean.
theorem reciprocalOrdCoeff_newton_determinant (N n : ℕ) (r : K) :
reciprocalOrdCoeff N n r = (n.factorial : K)⁻¹ *
(newtonHessenberg (fun k => r*kappaCoeff N k) n).det := by
have hb0 : reciprocalOrdCoeff N 0 r = 1 := by simp [reciprocalOrdCoeff]
have hb (i : ℕ) : ∑j ∈range (i+1), reciprocalOrdCoeff N j r *
(r*kappaCoeff N (i+1-j)) = (i+1 : K)*reciprocalOrdCoeff N (i+1) r := by
have h := reciprocalOrdCoeff_newton N r i
rw [Finset.Nat.sum_antidiagonal_eq_sum_range_succ_mk] at h
rw [h, mul_sum]
apply sum_congr rfl
intro j hj
have he : i-j+1=i+1-j := by have := mem_range.mp hj; omega
rw [he]
ring
rw [newtonHessenberg_det _ _ hb0 hb]
have hf : (n.factorial : K) ≠0 := by exact_mod_cast n.factorial_ne_zero
field_simp
The finite-dimensional convention includes 𝑛= 0, whose determinant and initial coefficient are one.
The formula uses ordinary coefficients. Obtaining the manuscript’s block coefficients requires their sep-
arate (𝑁𝑛)! conversion. The negative superdiagonal and the recurrence index are part of the checked
matrix, not inferred from a picture.
7.8 Row 150: hypergeometric normalization and summation
The literal denominator parameters are generated by removing the unique residue parameter equal to
one.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/HypergeometricAnalytic.lean.
def residueHypergeometricParameters (N j : ℕ) : Multiset ℂ:=
((range N).erase (N-j-1)).val.map (fun a => ((j+a+1 : ℕ) : ℂ)/(N : ℂ))
There are 𝑁−1 remaining parameters, all with positive real part. The source first relates factorials
to residue Pochhammer products. The analytic closure then converts the pinned Mathlib regularized
hypergeometric series to the ordinary normalization using Gamma factors and proves its sum.

Source: KomorebiPaperIV/HypergeometricAnalytic.lean.
theorem residueHypergeometric_hasSum {N j : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) (hj : j < N) (w : ℂ) :
HasSum (fun q => (residueZeroFCoeff N j q : ℂ) * w^q)
(residueHypergeometric N j w) := by
let b := residueHypergeometricParameters N j
have hb : ∀c ∈b, ∀k : ℕ, c ≠-(k : ℂ) := by
intro c hc k he
have hp := residueHypergeometricParameters_re_pos hN hc
rw [he] at hp
simp only [Complex.neg_re, Complex.natCast_re] at hp
have hk : (0 : ℝ) ≤k := Nat.cast_nonneg k
linarith
have hs := (Complex.regularizedHGFunSeries 0 b).hasSum (x := w) (by
rw [Complex.radius_regularizedHGFunSeries_eq_top (by simp)]
simp)

change HasSum _ (residueHypergeometric N j w) at h
apply h.congr_fun
intro q
simp only [Complex.regularizedHGFunSeries, FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars_apply_eq,
smul_eq_mul, ←mul_assoc, regularized_zeroF_normalization b hb]
rw [←residuePochhammerProduct_erase hN hj]
simp [residueZeroFCoeff]
Finally the entire transfer section is identified at arbitrary complex arguments.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/HypergeometricAnalytic.lean.
theorem entireTransfer_hypergeometric {N j : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N) (hj : j < N)
{omega : ℂ} (ho : IsPrimitiveRoot omega N) (lambda z : ℂ) :
entireTransfer N j omega lambda z =
(lambda*z)^j / (j.factorial : ℂ) *
residueHypergeometric N j ((lambda*z)^N / (N : ℂ)^N) := by
let f := fun m => PowerSeries.coeff m (transferSection N j lambda) * z^m
have hi : Function.Injective (fun q : ℕ=> N*q+j) := by
intro a b h
exact Nat.eq_of_mul_eq_mul_left hN (Nat.add_right_cancel h)
have hf : ∀m, m ∉Set.range (fun q : ℕ=> N*q+j) →f m = 0 := by
intro m hm
have he : m%N ≠j := by
intro he
apply hm
use m/N
change N*(m/N)+j=m
rw [←he, Nat.add_comm, Nat.mod_add_div]
simp [f, coeff_transferSection, transferSectionCoeff, he]
have ha := (hi.hasSum_iff hf).2 (hasSum_entireTransfer hN hj ho lambda z)
have hh := (residueHypergeometric_hasSum hN hj ((lambda*z)^N / (N : ℂ)^N)).mul_left
((lambda*z)^j / (j.factorial : ℂ))
apply ha.unique
apply hh.congr_fun
intro q

dsimp [Function.comp_def, f]
rw [coeff_transferSection, transferSectionCoeff,

The proof reindexes the original Taylor sum by 𝑁𝑞+ 𝑗, proves off-residue terms vanish, and matches
the factorial/Pochhammer coefficients. The argument retains the scale (𝜆𝑧)𝑁/𝑁𝑁and the prefactor
(𝜆𝑧)𝑗/𝑗!. For 𝑁= 1, the erased parameter multiset is empty and the same theorem applies.
7.9 Row 151: convergent quotient-algebra exponentials
The actual exponential series is defined in a topological algebra.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/QuotientExponential.lean.
def finiteAlgebraExponential {A : Type*} [Ring A] [Algebra ℂA]
[TopologicalSpace A] (x : A) (z : ℂ) : A :=
∑' m : ℕ, (z^m / (m.factorial : ℂ)) • x^m
Its definition alone does not assert summability. The next theorem supplies convergence for the cyclic
relation under the surrounding module’s topological linear-space assumptions.
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/QuotientExponential.lean.
theorem hasSum_cyclicAlgebra_exponential {N : ℕ} (hN : 0 < N)
{omega : ℂ} (ho : IsPrimitiveRoot omega N) (x : A) (hx : x^N = 1)
(lambda z : ℂ) :
HasSum (fun m : ℕ=> ((lambda*z)^m / (m.factorial : ℂ)) • x^m)
(∑j : Fin N, entireTransfer N j.val omega lambda z • x^j.val) := by
have h := hasSum_sum (s := Finset.univ) (fun j (_ : j ∈(Finset.univ : Finset (Fin N))) =>
(hasSum_entireTransfer hN j.isLt ho lambda z).smul_const (x^j.val))
apply h.congr_fun
intro m
have hxmod : x^m = x^(m%N) := by
calc
x^m = x^(m%N + N*(m/N)) := by rw [Nat.mod_add_div]
_ = x^(m%N) := by rw [pow_add, pow_mul, hx]; simp
rw [hxmod, Finset.sum_eq_single (⟨m%N, Nat.mod_lt m hN⟩: Fin N)]
· simp only [coeff_transferSection, transferSectionCoeff, ite_true]
congr 1
simp [mul_pow, div_eq_mul_inv, mul_assoc, mul_left_comm, mul_comm]
· intro j _ hj
have hne : m%N ≠j.val := by intro he; apply hj; exact Fin.ext he.symm
simp [coeff_transferSection, transferSectionCoeff, hne]
· simp
REALIZATION CALCULUS
25


OPERATION KOMOREBI
PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
The proof groups powers by residue and uses the scalar HasSum theorem for each coordinate. The
quotient algebras receive their finite-dimensional coefficient norm topologies and compatible normed-
space instances. The signed specialization is:
Source: KomorebiPaperIV/QuotientExponential.lean.
theorem signedAlgebra_pointwise_exponential {N : ℕ} [NeZero N]
{omega rho : ℂ} (ho : IsPrimitiveRoot omega N) (hr : rho^N = -1) (z : ℂ) :
finiteAlgebraExponential (signedCyclicGenerator ℂN) z =
∑j : Fin N, entireSigned N j.val omega rho z • (signedCyclicGenerator ℂN)^j.val := by
exact (hasSum_signedAlgebra_exponential (Nat.pos_of_ne_zero (NeZero.ne N)) ho hr
(signedCyclicGenerator ℂN) (signedCyclicGenerator_pow ℂN) z).tsum_eq
This is the final analytic link for result 6.2. The inherited formal power-series identity, the finite algebra
relation, and the pointwise convergent exponential all retain the same generator and normalization.

8 Reproduction, audit, and evidence handling
8.1 The exact environment
The canonical release is v1.0.0, dated 5 September 2026. Its toolchain file specifies leanprover/lean4:
v4.34.0-rc2. The recorded compiler commit is 6a10ac8c22beadecabdbb0919c2b50214762f91d. Mathlib
is pinned at aeb72f1e9981b6d35b426b16dca7759be3e7ba06. The complete nine-package revision table is
printed in the dependency appendix and preserved in lake-manifest.json.
The project has autoImplicit = false and relaxedAutoImplicit = false. Warnings are fatal in the
release build. The aggregate import integrates the development, while the explicit kernel audit checks
the mapped declarations and the namespace. Build caches are excluded from the release source.
8.2 What the saved kernel transcript establishes
The saved transcript contains one axiom record for each of the 1,164 mapped targets and ends with the
namespace audit’s pass line. The guide generator compares the target sets in four places: the theorem
map, the declaration-kind contract, the audit source’s explicit directives, and the transcript. It also
checks that every recorded axiom belongs to the three-item permitted set.
This reconciliation proves consistency of the preserved evidence files with the preserved source identi-
ties. It does not repeat elaboration, type checking, or a live environment traversal. The namespace totals
of 2,234 declarations and 1,999 theorem declarations are inherited results, not counts newly obtained
from a running Lean process. A lexical source parser cannot substitute for elaborated declaration types
or compiler-generated declarations.
8.3 Current checks for this guide
The generator validates every one of the 119 Lean source files against the canonical SHA-256 manifest.
It resolves the 1,164 target names to source declarations and checks the theorem-versus-construction
split against the explicit contract. It emits the complete row catalog, 68-result ledger, direct-import
inventory, exact pins, and source-excerpt manifest.
The source policy scan excludes comments and string literals before checking for forbidden proof place-
holders and unsafe shortcuts. The kernel-audit file is treated separately because its exclusion logic nec-
essarily names prohibited axioms. This is a source-hygiene check. It does not establish that elaboration
succeeds.
No new Lean build was available for this companion edition. The 43 declarations in SemanticChecks
.lean remain source-level and inherited execution evidence here. They are not relabeled as 43 newly
executed tests. The companion edition changes explanatory material and navigation, not the canonical
Lean files.
8.4 Replay the original release gate
Use a disposable copy of the canonical PRSC_IV directory with the pinned Lean toolchain and dependen-
cies available. The documented release gate requires Lean and checksums, including a full compiler
replay.
bash VERIFY.sh --release
If the original release archive is available, its exact archive-entry checks can be included:
REALIZATION CALCULUS
27


OPERATION KOMOREBI
PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
bash VERIFY.sh --release --archive /path/to/PRSC_IV_v1.0.0.zip
The new three-companion edition archive is not the original canonical release ZIP. Do not pass it to a
gate that expects the original release’s exact entry set.
The gate also accepts a validated external dependency cache:
bash VERIFY.sh --release --lean-packages-cache /path/to/packages
The cache must match the pinned package revisions. The release checker requires a clean project with-
out a preexisting local build cache. It creates and cleans its local attachment under its own rules. A
fresh extracted copy is the clearest way to avoid mixing source verification with prior build state.
For a focused manual inspection from formalization/lean, the relevant commands include:
lake --wfail build
lake env lean -E warning KomorebiPaperIV/KernelAudit.lean
These are replay instructions, not commands claimed to have run in this companion edition. The
full release gate additionally checks exact kinds and manuscript associations, source policy, document
reconstruction, checksums, and archive properties when requested.
8.5 Rebuild this guide without running Lean
The supplied build.py compiles the existing guide with four XeLaTeX passes. It does not regenerate
its data, install packages, invoke Lean, or modify the canonical source. Run it from the guide directory:
python3 build.py
The optional static-data generator is separate. Its defaults fit the edition’s packaged folder layout, and
its paths can be overridden:
python3 build_guide_data.py \
--source /path/to/PRSC_IV \
--traditional /path/to/editable/traditional
python3 build.py
It writes generated navigation and provenance inside the guide directory. The code excerpts have both
untouched UTF-8 text and display-escaped copies. Display escaping preserves the source characters
while allowing the chosen fonts and verbatim environment to render Lean notation. The raw excerpts
are the authoritative source intervals.
8.6 Failure modes worth recognizing
A dependency revision mismatch means the attempted replay is not the pinned release environment.
A saved transcript whose target set differs from the contract is incomplete evidence even if its final line
says pass. A declaration that is present lexically but fails to elaborate has not passed a new formal run.
A PDF that builds successfully says nothing by itself about the Lean proofs.
The inherited computational audit also reports that an older strengthened numerical suite accepted
mutations to a projector identity and a zero-counting constant. Its reported pass totals therefore cannot
certify theorems. The canonical formal contract and kernel evidence should not be replaced by those
historical finite-test totals. Finite checks can still detect selected regressions, but their sensitivity and
scope must be stated.

PAPER IV / LEAN GUIDE
8.7 How to inspect one result end to end
Choose a manuscript record in the next ledger. Read all of its map rows, including later rows that close
analytic clauses. Follow each exact declaration name to its source location. Inspect the surrounding
variables, typeclass instances, definitions, and imported lemmas. Check the target’s kind in the contract
and its axiom record in the saved transcript. If a new formal run is needed, use the pinned environment
and retain its complete output separately from inherited evidence.
For example, result 4.5 joins row 27’s factorial/Pochhammer formal identity with row 150’s literal pa-
rameter multiset, Gamma normalization, and actual convergent hypergeometric summation. Review-
ing only row 27 would miss completed analytic coverage. Reviewing only the final identity would
miss the coefficient normalization on which it depends. The contract is designed to make both routes
visible.
